On the hydrostatic limit of stably stratified fluids with isopycnal diffusivity
Roberta Bianchini, Vincent Duchêne

Abstract
. This article is concerned with rigorously justifying the hydrostatic limit for continuously stratified incompressible fluids under the influence of gravity. The main distinction of this work compared to previous studies is the absence of any (regularizing) viscosity contribution added to the fluid-dynamics equations; only thickness diffusivity effects are considered. Motivated by applications to oceanography, the diffusivity effects in this work arise from an additional advection term, the specific form of which was proposed by Gent and McWilliams in the 1990s to model the effective contributions of geostrophic eddy correlations in non-eddy-resolving systems. The results of this paper heavily rely on the assumption of stable stratification. We establish the well-posedness of the hydrostatic equations and the original (non-hydrostatic) equations for stably stratified fluids, along with their convergence in the limit of vanishing shallow-water parameter. These results are obtained in high but finite Sobolev regularity and carefully account for the various parameters involved. A key element of our analysis is the reformulation of the systems using isopycnal coordinates, enabling us to provide meticulous energy estimates that are not readily apparent in the original Eulerian coordinate system.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q35, 76B03, 76B70, 76M45, 76U60, 86A05

1.
Introduction

The following system describes the evolution of heterogeneous incompressible flows under the influence of gravity,

(1.1)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\rho+({\bm{u}}+{\bm{u}}_{\star})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\rho+(w+w_{\star}){\partial}_{z}\rho

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\rho\big({\partial}_{t}{\bm{u}}+(({\bm{u}}+{\bm{u}}_{\star})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}){\bm{u}}+(w+w_{\star}){\partial}_{z}{\bm{u}}\big)+\nabla_{\bm{x}}P

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\rho\big({\partial}_{t}w+({\bm{u}}+{\bm{u}}_{\star})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}w+(w+w_{\star}){\partial}_{z}w\big)+{\partial}_{z}P+g\,\rho

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}+{\partial}_{z}w

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle P|_{z=\zeta}-P_{\rm atm}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\zeta+({\bm{u}}+{\bm{u}}_{\star})|_{z=\zeta}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\zeta-(w+w_{\star})|_{z=\zeta}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle w|_{z=-H}

\displaystyle=0.

Here,
t
and
({\bm{x}},z)
are the time, and horizontal-vertical space variables, and we denote by
\nabla_{\bm{x}},\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot,\Delta_{\bm{x}}
the gradient, divergence and Laplacian with respect to
{\bm{x}}
. The vector field
({\bm{u}},w)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
is the (horizontal and vertical) velocity,
\rho>0
is the density,
P\in\mathbb{R}
is the incompressible pressure, all being defined in the spatial domain

\Omega_{t}=\{({\bm{x}},z)\ :\ {\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ -H<z<\zeta(t,{\bm{x}})\},

where
\zeta(t,{\bm{x}})
describes the location of a free surface, and
H
is the depth of the layer at rest. The gravity field is assumed to be constant and vertical, and
g>0
is the gravity acceleration constant. Finally, the advection terms associated with the ’bolus velocity’
({\bm{u}}_{\star},w_{\star})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
were proposed by Gent and McWilliams
[
GMCW90
]
. These terms are introduced to account for the contribution of geostrophic eddy correlations to the effective transport velocities in non-eddy-resolving (large-scale) models. Their specific forms in the simplest case of constant diffusivity parameter
\kappa
read as follows

(1.2)

{\bm{u}}_{\star}=\kappa{\partial}_{z}\left(\frac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}\rho}{{\partial}_{z}\rho}\right)\ ,\quad w_{\star}=-\kappa\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot\left(\frac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}\rho}{{\partial}_{z}\rho}\right)\,,\qquad\kappa>0\,.

Discarding the effective advection terms (i.e. setting
\kappa=0
), one recovers the Euler equations for heterogeneous incompressible fluids under the influence of vertical gravity forces, where the last two lines of (
1.1
) model the kinematic equation at the free surface and the impermeability condition of the rigid bottom respectively.

In (
1.1
), the pressure
P
can be recovered from its (atmospheric) value at the surface,
P_{\rm atm}
, by solving the elliptic boundary-value problem induced by the incompressibility constraint of divergence-free velocity fields. Yet in
the shallow-water regime, where the horizontal scale of the perturbation is large compared with the depth of the layer
H
, formal computations (see below) suggest that vertical accelerations can be neglected and that the pressure
P
approximately satisfies the hydrostatic balance law, that is

(1.3)

{\partial}_{z}P+g\,\rho=0.

Replacing the equation for the vertical velocity in (
1.1
) by the identity in (
1.3
) yields the so-called
hydrostatic equations
:

(1.4)

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\rho+({\bm{u}}+{\bm{u}}_{\star})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\rho+(w+w_{\star}){\partial}_{z}\rho

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\rho\big({\partial}_{t}{\bm{u}}+(({\bm{u}}+{\bm{u}}_{\star})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}){\bm{u}}+(w+w_{\star}){\partial}_{z}{\bm{u}}\big)+\nabla_{\bm{x}}P

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\zeta+({\bm{u}}+{\bm{u}}_{\star})|_{z=\zeta}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\zeta-(w+w_{\star})|_{z=\zeta}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle=P_{\rm atm}+g\int_{z}^{\zeta}\rho(z^{\prime},\cdot)\dd z^{\prime},

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=-\int_{-H}^{z}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}(z^{\prime},\cdot)\dd z^{\prime}.

Our aim in this work is to rigorously justify the hydrostatic equations (
1.4
) as an asymptotic model for the non-hydrostatic equations (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) in the shallow-water regime, for regular and stably stratified flows.

1.1.
The “bolus” velocities in geophysical flows

Let us now delve into the physical motivation behind introducing additional transport velocities,
{\bm{u}}_{\star}
and
w_{\star}
, defined in (
1.2
), into the systems of equations (
1.1
) and (
1.4
). As mentioned earlier, these “bolus” velocities serve as modifications to the standard incompressible Euler equations, whether with or without the hydrostatic assumption, derived from fundamental principles of fluid mechanics. In this regard, they play a role analogous to the viscosity contributions in the Navier–Stokes equations. Concerning the latter, it is worth pointing out that in theoretical and laboratory studies on density-stratified geophysical flows, viscosity effects do not model molecular viscosity but rather “turbulent” or “eddy” viscosities.
It is important to mention that in the shallow-water regime, where horizontal scales are larger than vertical scales, the non-dimensionalization of standard molecular viscosity contributions results in anisotropic viscosity terms that are predominant in the vertical direction. In contrast, turbulent viscosity is widely reported to be predominant in the horizontal (or more precisely isopycnal) direction; see, for example,
[
Griffies2003
]
*Section 17.6. In this work, we choose to neglect viscosity effects entirely and instead focus on eddy-induced diffusivity.

The deterministic modeling of effective diffusivity induced by eddy correlation that we adopt in this work takes its roots in the 1990s and is due to Gent & McWilliams
[
GMCW90
]
, see also
[
GMCW95
,
GMCW96
]
.
Motivated by the need to model the averaged dissipative effects of mesoscale eddies, which were not computationally feasible to resolve at that time, on the large-scale flow at the macroscopic level, they consider unknowns
(\rho,{\bm{u}},w)
as the large-scale components of the density and velocity field, respectively. The approach involves introducing suitable correctors to the mass conservation equation and equations for tracers (such as salinity and temperature). Specifically, they suggest incorporating bolus velocity fields
{\bm{u}}_{\star}
and
w_{\star}
, defined in (
1.2
).
Adding the bolus velocity contribution in the momentum conservation equation was suggested in
[
GMCW96
,
Gent01
]
as well.
1
1

1

Let us point out that our analysis would hold (and would in fact be simpler) without the contributions
{\bm{u}}_{\star}
and
w_{\star}
in the evolution equations for the velocity. We add these terms because we believe they are important from a modeling point of view, and would play a crucial role in a refined analysis of the large-time behavior and/or less regular (weak) solutions.
One of the main ingredient leading to the specific form of the bolus velocity fields is that the averaged dissipative effect of mesoscale eddies should act predominantly along isopycnals sheets (that is along and not across surfaces of equal densities).
It should be mentioned that the Gent & McWilliams eddy-induced advection is only part of the subgridscale parametrization of mesoscale eddies used in global ocean general circulation models, which also include Redi’s eddy-induced diffusion
[
Redi82
]
. Specifically, the equations for tracers including the so-called Gent–McWilliams–Redi eddy parametrization read (see
[
KornTiti
]
and references therein)

(1.5)

\partial_{t}C+({\bm{U}}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{3})C=\nabla_{3}\cdot\big(K_{\rm R}\nabla_{3}C\big)+\nabla_{3}\cdot\big(K_{\rm GM}\nabla_{3}C\big),

where
C
is a tracer (typically the temperature
\theta
and the salinity
S
),
{\bm{U}}_{3}:=({\bm{u}},w)
denotes the three-dimensional velocity field, and
\nabla_{3}
the three-dimensional gradient. Above, we define
K_{\rm R}
and
K_{\rm GM}
by

K_{\rm R}:=\frac{K_{I}}{1+|{\bm{L}}|^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1+L_{y}^{2}&-L_{x}L_{y}&L_{x}\\
-L_{x}L_{y}&1+L_{x}^{2}&L_{y}\\
L_{x}&L_{y}&|{\bm{L}}|^{2}\end{pmatrix}+\frac{K_{D}}{1+|{\bm{L}}|^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}L_{x}^{2}&L_{x}L_{y}&-L_{x}\\
L_{x}L_{y}&L_{y}^{2}&-L_{y}\\
-L_{x}&-L_{y}&1\end{pmatrix}

K_{\rm GM}:=\kappa\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-L_{x}\\
0&0&-L_{y}\\
L_{x}&L_{y}&0\end{pmatrix}

where we used the notation
{\bm{L}}:=(L_{x},L_{y})=\frac{-\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\rho}{\partial_{z}\rho}
, and the non-negative constants
K_{I}
and
K_{D}
are the isoneutral and dianeutral diffusivity coefficients. Notice that the tensor
K_{\rm R}
is symmetric, while the tensor
K_{\rm GM}
is skew-symmetric, so that the dissipative effect due to Gent & McWilliams eddy-advection is not obvious. In fact, there has been some debate about the adiabatic nature of the Gent & McWilliams parametrization
[
Gent11
]
. As it is evident from the formulation of the equations in isopycnal coordinates (see equations (
1.8
) below), the Gent & McWilliams eddy-induced advection terms introduce a diffusive contribution to the thickness variable. This is why the parameter
\kappa
is often referred to as the
thickness diffusivity
parameter.

Another important aspect is that, when applied to the density variable, the isoneutral contribution of Redi’s eddy-induced diffusion vanishes exactly, while the dianeutral contribution becomes an isotropic three-dimensional Laplacian, and the Gent & McWilliams contribution can be interpreted as advection. Consequently, in situations where the equation of state
\rho=\rho(\theta,S)
is a linear combination of the tracers (with equal diffusivity parameters for all tracers), the equations (
1.5
) yield

\partial_{t}\rho+({\bm{U}}_{3}\cdot\nabla_{3})\rho+({\bm{U}}_{3}^{\star}\cdot\nabla_{3})\rho=K_{D}\Delta_{3}\rho,

where we denote
{\bm{U}}_{3}^{\star}:=({\bm{u}}^{\star},w^{\star})
defined in (
1.2
).
Since the dianeutral diffusivity coefficient is typically set much smaller than the isoneutral diffusivity coefficient,
0\leq K_{D}\ll K_{I}
, it makes sense to neglect the right-hand side of the above equation. This results in the mass conservation equations in (
1.1
) and (
1.4
).

Let us emphasize that our framework leaves aside many important ingredients which are usually considered in the so-called primitive equations modeling large-scale flows (see
e.g.

[
Griffies2003
]
), and which are in fact responsible for the emergence of mesoscale eddies that led to the Gent–McWilliams–Redi eddy parametrization: typically the rotational effects, vertical boundaries, bathymetry and nonlinear equation of state.
As such neither the non-hydrostatic system (
1.1
) nor the hydrostatic system (
1.4
) can be considered as relevant global ocean models.
This work is motivated by studying on a theoretical grounds the interplay between (stable) stratification, hydrostatic (shallow water) limits, and the eddy-induced thickness diffusivity contributions.
Many of these aforementioned constituents which are essential to modeling aspects are not necessarily important in the mathematical analysis, and could be easily incorporated in our study at the price of blurring the main mechanisms that we would like to pinpoint, while interesting singular limits (geostrophic balance, boundary layers,
etc.
) would of course require and deserve a specific treatment.

Let us finally mention that there exists a huge mathematical literature dedicated to the investigation of fluid-dynamics equations in the probabilistic setting, where the cumulative effect of mesoscale eddies on the large-scale flow is modeled by means of suitable (additive or multiplicative) noises. For that context, we refer to
[
flandoliGL2021
,
flandoliGL2021-2
,
ChapronCrisanHolmEtAl23
,
ChapronCrisanHolmEtAl24
]
, while our setting will be completely deterministic.

1.2.
Two-velocity hydrodynamics in other contexts

Interestingly, equations involving two distinct velocities have been proposed and studied independently, in different contexts. We refer, for instance, to a series of works initiated by Brenner in
[
Brenner04
,
Brenner05
]
(see also
[
Brenner12
]
) on compressible barotropic flows, which are described by the following equations:

(1.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\varrho{\bm{u}})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\kappa\nabla_{\bm{x}}\varrho),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\varrho{\bm{u}})+\di_{\bm{x}}\big(\varrho{\bm{u}}\otimes({\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}\varrho}{\varrho})\Big)+\nabla_{\bm{x}}p

\displaystyle=\di_{\bm{x}}\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}}),

where the convention for the divergence
\di_{\bm{x}}
is such that
\di_{\bm{x}}({\bm{u}}\otimes{\bm{v}})=({\bm{v}}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}){\bm{u}}+(\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{v}}){\bm{u}}
, and
\mathbb{S}
is the standard Newtonian viscous stress:

(1.7)

\mathbb{S}(\nabla_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}})=\mu\big(\nabla_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}}+\nabla_{\bm{x}}^{\rm T}{\bm{u}}\big)+\lambda(\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}})\Id.

Moreover,
\varrho
(resp.
{\bm{u}}
) represents the density (resp. velocity), and
p=p(\varrho)
is the given pressure function.
The system (
1.6
) proposes additional diffusive contributions to the standard barotropic Navier–Stokes equation. Remarkably, these additional terms bear a striking resemblance to the Gent & McWilliams “bolus velocity” advection when expressed in isopycnal coordinates. Indeed, anticipating the derivation in Section
2.1
, the hydrostatic system (
1.4
) may be reformulated as

(1.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(h{\bm{u}})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\kappa\nabla_{\bm{x}}h),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(h{\bm{u}})+\di_{\bm{x}}\big(\varrho{\bm{u}}\otimes({\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{h})\Big)+h\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi

\displaystyle=0,

where
h
represents the infinitesimal thickness of pycnoclines, and the explicit expression of the Montgomery potential
\psi
is given below. Despite their apparent similarity, there are important differences between (
1.6
) and (
1.8
), in addition from the obvious fact that the viscous stress is discarded in the latter (
\mu=\lambda=0
).
In (
1.6
), the space variable
{\bm{x}}
is typically three-dimensional, whereas in analogous situations, it is only two-dimensional in (
1.8
). However, the variables
h
and
{\bm{u}}
therein depend on an additional “density” variable, which labels pycnoclines based on their vertical distribution. Importantly, the contribution
\psi
is not expressed as a function of
h
but rather as a linear operator within this infinite-dimensional framework:

(1.9)

\psi(t,{\bm{x}},r)=g\int_{\rho_{0}}^{r}\min(1,r^{\prime}/r)h(t,{\bm{x}},r^{\prime})\dd r^{\prime}.

In particular, a crucial difference between (
1.6
) and (
1.8
) is that, in the absence of any diffusive and viscous effect (
\kappa=\mu=\lambda=0
), the initial-value problem for (
1.6
) is locally-in-time well-posed in Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
when
s>d/2+1
for initial data satisfying the non-vacuum condition
\varrho\geq c_{0}>0
and provided that the pressure function is sufficiently regular and satisfies
p^{\prime}(\varrho)>0
, while the well-posedness of the initial-value problem for (
1.8
) in finite-regularity spaces is an open problem, as we discuss below.

Notwithstanding this fact we can remark that, from a mathematical point of view, the diffusive contributions (
\kappa>0
) provide a very valuable regularization mechanism which remedies issues encountered in the mathematical theory (in particular the global-in-time existence of weak solutions) of the standard Navier–Stokes system, that is the absence of uniform bounds on the density
\varrho
, the possibility of the appearance of vacuum regions, and the possible development of uncontrollable oscillations for the density due to the low regularity of the velocity field; see
[
FeireislVasseur10
,
FeireislGwiazdaSwierczewska-GwiazdaEtAl16
]
. In fact, motivated by the construction of suitable regularizations of the compressible Euler equations (in the baroclinic framework), Guermond and Popov derived independently a generalization of (
1.6
) without invoking phenomenological assumptions
[
GuermondPopov14
]
.

Interestingly, systems similar to (
1.6
) arise also in relation with compressible barotropic flows with degenerate viscosities (which coincides when
p(\rho)\propto\rho^{2}
with the viscous shallow water equations advocated by Gent in
[
Gent93
]
and derived from the Navier–Stokes equations in
[
GerbeauPerthame01
,
Marche07
,
BreschNoble07
]
, and to a variant of the the Aw–Rascle model for traffic flows studied in
[
ChaudhuriGwiazdaZatorska23
]
in the pressureless case; similar models also appear in quantum hydrodynamics, see
[
Juengel10
]
). Indeed, restricting to spatial dimension
d=1
, discarding the Newtonian viscous stress (
\mu=\lambda=0
), and denoting
v=u-\kappa\frac{{\partial}_{x}\varrho}{\varrho}
, we find after straightforward computations that (
1.6
) reads

(1.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho+{\partial}_{x}(\varrho v)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\varrho v)+{\partial}_{x}\big(\varrho v^{2}\big)+\nabla_{\bm{x}}p

\displaystyle={\partial}_{x}(\kappa\varrho{\partial}_{x}v).

In the above, the diffusive contributions act as a degenerate viscosity, in the sense that the variable viscosity coefficient vanishes when the density
\varrho
vanishes. In their analysis of such systems (and generalizations thereof), Bresch and Desjardins introduced the so-called BD entropy in
[
BreschDesjardins03
,
BreschDesjardins04
]
. In fact this BD entropy stems from a reformulation of systems of the form (
1.10
) (in higher dimension) with viscous stresses as in (
1.7
) but with variable coefficients
\lambda(\varrho)
and
\mu(\varrho)
, under a form analogous to (
1.6
). This is made possible thanks to a key cancellation when a special algebraic relation between
\lambda(\varrho)
and
\mu(\varrho)
holds, namely
\lambda(\varrho)=2(\varrho\mu^{\prime}(\varrho)-\mu(\varrho))
. The system (
1.10
) corresponds to the special case
\mu(\rho)=\frac{1}{2}\kappa\varrho
and
\lambda(\varrho)=0
initially considered in
[
BreschDesjardinsLin03
]
. This discovery triggered a series of work, including
[
MelletVasseur07
,
VasseurYu16
,
BreschDesjardinsZatorska15
,
BreschVasseurYu22
]
(see also references therein) which address the global existence of weak solutions to the barotropic Navier–Stokes equations with degenerate viscosities. In
[
BreschDesjardinsZatorska15
,
BreschVasseurYu22
]
, the authors extend the BD entropy to a so-called
\kappa
-entropy, considering the equation for the velocity
v+\theta\kappa\frac{{\partial}_{x}\varrho}{\varrho}
together with the equation for
v
(where
\theta\in(0,1)
is a free parameter, which is denoted
\kappa
in
[
BreschDesjardinsZatorska15
]
). This leads to an entropy depending on the parameter
\theta
, where the choice
\theta=0
corresponds to the standard energy associated with (
1.10
), the choice
\theta=1
corresponds to the BD entropy, while the choice
\theta=1/2
was considered in
[
GisclonLacroix-Violet15
]
. In all these works, the crucial matter consists in exploiting some compactness on the density variable
\varrho
gained from the parabolic nature of the evolution equation for the density when written with suitable modified velocities so as to prevent, in particular, the appearance of vacuum regions. Let us clarify again that our analysis concerns local-in-time regular solutions, and that for this matter the diffusivity-induced regularizing mechanism that we exploit is much less subtle than in the previously mentioned works, while the key difficulties in our analysis stem from the pressure contributions, namely (
1.9
) in (
1.8
).

Finally, we would like to highlight the works
[
CoudercDuranVila17
,
DuranVilaBaraille17
]
, in which the authors introduce (artificially) diffusive contributions analogous to those in (
1.6
) at the discrete level for the multilayer shallow water system. This is done with the aim of developing numerical schemes that ensure control over the discrete total energy.

2
2

2

The effective velocities are defined slightly differently in
[
CoudercDuranVila17
,
DuranVilaBaraille17
]
compared with (
1.8
),
since the analogous fully continuous equations read

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(h{\bm{u}})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\kappa\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(h{\bm{u}})+\di_{\bm{x}}\big(\varrho{\bm{u}}\otimes({\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi}{h})\Big)+h\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi

\displaystyle=0,

where
\psi
is defined in (
1.9
). The multilayer shallow water system can be viewed as a system of several equations of the form (
1.6
), coupled through the pressure contributions. Alternatively it may be interpreted as a semi-discretized (with respect to the density variable) version of the system (
1.8
), as rigorously shown in
[
Adim
]
.

1.3.
Results on the hydrostatic equations and hydrostatic limit

The initial-value problem for the hydrostatic equations (
1.4
) (and (
1.8
)-(
1.9
)) without diffusivity or viscosity contributions is not well-posed in finite-regularity functional spaces in general. In fact, restricting to homogeneous flows (that is
\rho
being constant), ill-posedness was established by Renardy
[
Renardy09
]
at the linear level, and by Han-Kwan and Nguyen
[
HanKwanNguyen16
]
at the nonlinear level. Yet if we additionally assume that the initial data satisfy the Rayleigh condition of (strict) convexity/concavity in the vertical direction, well-posedness is restored
[
Brenier99
,
Grenier99
,
MasmoudiWong12
]
.
The picture is very different in the framework of stably stratified flows. The celebrated Miles and Howard criterion
[
Miles61
,
Howard61
]
states that the linearized equations about equilibria
(\underline{\rho}(z),\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}(z))
do not exhibit unstable modes (in dimension
d=1
, see
[
Gallay
]
*Remark 1.3 when
d=2
) provided that the local Richardson number is greater than 1/4 everywhere, that is

\forall z\in[-H,0],\qquad|\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}(z)|^{2}\leq 4g\left(\frac{-\underline{\rho}^{\prime}(z)}{\underline{\rho}(z)}\right).

Notice that the stabilizing (resp. destabilizing) effect of the stable stratification (resp. shear velocity) is clearly encoded by the above criterion.
However, let us underline again that the local
3
3

3

Incidentally, notice that it was proven that smooth solutions to the hydrostatic equations in the homogeneous framework can develop singularities in finite time; see
[
CaoIbrahimNakanishiEtAl15
,
Wong15
]
, and
[
IbrahimLinTiti21
]
in the presence of rotation. Again, such a result is not known in the stably stratified setting. Our study will be limited to local-in-time solutions.
well-posedness of the (nonlinear) hydrostatic equations in finite regularity spaces, even for initial data (strictly) satisfying the above inequality, is an open problem.

This is in sharp contrast with the available results on the non-hydrostatic equations. In this context, we mention the recent work by Desjardins, Lannes and Saut
[
Desjardins-Lannes-Saut
]
,
which is the closest to our framework and provides the well-posedness of the (inviscid and non-diffusive) non-hydrostatic equations in Sobolev spaces (using the rigid-lid assumption). Even though the stabilizing effect of the stable stratification is also a key ingredient of that work, it is not powerful enough to prove that the lifespan of the solutions to the non-hydrostatic equations
is uniform with respect to the shallow-water parameter measuring the ratio of vertical to horizontal lengths, without additional smallness conditions on the initial data. A more detailed comparison between
[
Desjardins-Lannes-Saut
]
and our results is provided in Section
2.3
below.

From the technical viewpoint, the reason of this discrepancy – in terms of the available results – between the non-hydrostatic and hydrostatic equations is that the vertical velocity variable
w
changes its role passing from prognostic (when it belongs to the set of unknowns) to diagnostic (when it is reconstructed from the unknowns), whence losing one order of regularity; see the fourth equation in (
1.4
). In an analogous way, the available control on the pressure contributions lose one derivative with respect to the horizontal space variable between the non-hydrostatic and hydrostatic systems. One of the main observations in
[
Desjardins-Lannes-Saut
]
and in our work is that for stably stratified flows the equations benefit from a symmetric structure which can be exploited to partially (but not fully) overcome the difficulties related to these loss of derivatives. In this way we are able to pinpoint contributions which may generate high-frequency instabilities, revealing clearly the destabilizing influence of shear velocities.

In order to deal with loss of derivatives without restricting the analysis to the analytic setting as in
[
KukavicaTemamVicolEtAl11
,
PaicuZhangZhang20
]
, one natural approach is the introduction of (regularizing) viscosity and diffusivity contributions.
This is the framework of most of the theoretical studies concerning the hydrostatic equations and/or the hydrostatic limit, starting with the work
of Azérad and Guillén
[
AzeradGuillen2001
]
. A landmark in the theory is the work of Cao and Titi
[
CaoTiti07
]
where the
global well-posedness of the initial-value problem for the hydrostatic equations was proved in dimension
d+1=3
: this striking result should be compared with the state of the art concerning the Navier-Stokes equations.
Several mathematical studies were later established to investigate situations involving partial viscosities and diffusivities, as well as more physically relevant boundary conditions.
Rather than providing an extensive bibliography for this huge set of results, we limit ourselves to point out the works
[
CT2014
,
CT2016
]
, which extended the previous results to the case where only horizontal viscosity and diffusivity are added to the equations.
We also mention
[
LiTiti2019
,
FurukawaGigaHieberEtAl20
,
LiTitiYuan21
]
(in the homogeneous case) and
[
PuZhou21
,
PuZhou22
]
(in the heterogeneous case) for recent results on the hydrostatic limit and an extended list of references (therein).

A specificity of our analysis with respect to the previous ones (with the exception of
[
Desjardins-Lannes-Saut
]
) is that we shall crucially
use
the (stable) density stratification assumption, but we completely neglect viscosity-induced regularization and only allow for thickness diffusivity effects. We shall also keep track of all relevant parameters in our estimates, and in particular we use diffusivity-induced regularization only when crucially needed. This allows to characterize the relevant convergence rates and timescales, and to exhibit a balance between the destabilizing effect of shear velocities and the stabilizing result of thickness diffusivity.
Moreover, to the best of our knowledge,
this is the first rigorous mathematical study where the specific form of the diffusivity contributions, due to Gent and McWilliams
[
GMCW90
]
and modeling effective diffusivity induced by eddy correlation, is taken into account, with the exception of the work of Korn and Titi
[
KornTiti
]
which appeared after our work was completed. In
[
KornTiti
]
, the authors extend the work
[
CaoTiti07
]
to a framework with the full Redi-Gent-McWilliams parametrization and nonlinear equation of state, pointing out the necessity of using a suitably regularized density in the definitions of the diffusion operators in (
1.5
). Such regularization is not necessary in our analysis, which differs by two main ingredients. Firstly, as previously mentioned, we restrict ourselves to stably stratified flows and local-in-time regular solutions. Secondly, we will consider the equations (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) in the isopycnal coordinate system.

1.4.
Plan of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we first rewrite the systems of equations (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) with bolus velocities (
1.2
) in isopycnal coordinates. This yields systems (
2.1
) and (
2.5
), respectively. We then present the main results of our work, namely Theorem
2.1
on the initial-value problem for the hydrostatic equations (
2.5
), and Theorem
2.2
on the strong convergence of solutions to the non-hydrostatic equations (
2.1
) towards corresponding solutions to the hydrostatic equations (
2.5
), as the shallow-water parameter vanishes.

Section
3
is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
2.1
.

In Section
4
, we analyze the non-hydrostatic equations. We first provide elliptic estimates for the boundary-value problem of the pressure reconstruction (Lemma
4.1
and Corollary
4.2
), and use them to infer two partial results concerning the initial-value problem: Proposition
4.3
(restricted to small time) and Proposition
4.8
(restricted to small data).

In Section
5
, we bypass these restrictions assuming sufficiently small values of the shallow-water parameter, and complete the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Finally, in Appendix
A
we provide product, commutator and composition estimates in anisotropic Sobolev spaces which are of independent interest.

2.
Main results

2.1.
The model in isopycnal coordinates and non-dimensionalization

Let us consider smooth solutions to (
1.1
) defined on a time interval
I_{t}
. Assuming that the flow is
stably stratified
, i.e.

\inf(-{\partial}_{z}\rho)>0,

the density
\rho:z\rightarrow\rho(\cdot,\cdot,z)
is an invertible function of
z
. We denote its inverse
\eta:\varrho\rightarrow\eta(\cdot,\cdot,\varrho)
, so that

\rho(t,{\bm{x}},\eta(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho))=\varrho,\quad\eta(t,{\bm{x}},\rho(t,{\bm{x}},z))=z.

We also assume that
\rho(t,{\bm{x}},-H)=\rho_{1},\;\rho(t,{\bm{x}},\zeta(t,{\bm{x}}))=\rho_{0}
for
(t,{\bm{x}})\in I_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}
are two fixed and positive constant reference densities. Then we have

(2.1)

\eta:I_{t}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\quad\text{ with }\quad\Omega:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})\quad\text{ and }\quad h:=-\partial_{\varrho}\eta>0,

the latter inequality accounting for the stable stratification assumption.
We now introduce

\check{\bm{u}}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)={\bm{u}}(t,{\bm{x}},\eta(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)),\quad\check{w}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)=w(t,{\bm{x}},\eta(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)),\quad\check{P}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)=P(t,{\bm{x}},\eta(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)).

From the chain rule, we infer that
system (
1.1
) in isopycnal coordinates reads

(2.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta+\check{\bm{u}}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta-\check{w}

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}\eta,

\displaystyle\varrho\Big(\partial_{t}\check{\bm{u}}+\big((\check{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{h}\big)\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big)\check{\bm{u}}\Big)+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\check{P}+\frac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta}{h}\partial_{\varrho}\check{P}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\varrho\Big(\partial_{t}\check{w}+\big(\check{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{h}\big)\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\check{w}\Big)-\frac{{\partial}_{\varrho}\check{P}}{h}+g\varrho

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle-h\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot\check{\bm{u}}-(\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta)\cdot(\partial_{\varrho}\check{\bm{u}})+\partial_{\varrho}\check{w}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\check{P}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}=P_{\rm atm},\qquad\check{w}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{1}}

\displaystyle=0.

Notice that differentiating with respect to
\varrho
the first equation and using the fourth equation (stemming from the incompressibility constraint), the mass conservation reads

(2.3)

\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(h\check{\bm{u}})=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}h.

At this point, we are ready to introduce a dimensionless version of the previous system.
We are interested in deviations from steady solutions to the incompressible Euler equations with variable density:

({h_{\rm eq}},{\bm{u}}_{\rm eq},w_{\rm eq},P_{\rm eq})=({\underline{h}(\varrho)},\underline{{\bm{u}}}(\varrho),0,\underline{P}(\varrho)),

which satisfy the equilibrium condition

{\partial}_{\varrho}\underline{P}(\varrho)=g\varrho\underline{h}(\varrho).

Therefore, we consider (non-necessarily small) fluctuations of that steady solution, so that our unknowns admit the following decomposition:

\displaystyle h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)

\displaystyle{=\underline{h}(\varrho)+h_{\rm pert}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)},

\displaystyle\quad\check{\bm{u}}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)

\displaystyle=\underline{{\bm{u}}}(\varrho)+{\bm{u}}_{\rm pert}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho),

\displaystyle\check{w}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)

\displaystyle=0+w_{\rm pert}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho),

\displaystyle\quad\check{P}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)

\displaystyle=\underline{P}(\varrho)+P_{\rm pert}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho).

Furthermore, we non-dimensionalize the equations through the following scaled variables: we set

\frac{h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)}{H}=\tilde{\underline{h}}(\varrho)+\tilde{h}(\tilde{t},\tilde{\bm{x}},\varrho)\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{\check{\bm{u}}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)}{\sqrt{gH}}=\tilde{\underline{{\bm{u}}}}(\varrho)+\tilde{\bm{u}}(\tilde{t},\tilde{\bm{x}},\varrho),

and
4
4

4

Notice the different scaling between the horizontal and vertical velocity fields. There,
\lambda
is a reference horizontal length.

\frac{\lambda}{H}\frac{\check{w}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)}{\sqrt{gH}}=\tilde{w}(\tilde{t},\tilde{\bm{x}},\varrho),\quad\frac{\check{P}(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)}{gH}=\frac{P_{\rm atm}}{gH}+\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\varrho}\varrho^{\prime}\tilde{\underline{h}}(\varrho^{\prime})\,d\varrho^{\prime}+\tilde{P}(\tilde{t},\tilde{\bm{x}},\varrho),

where we use the following scaled coordinates
5
5

5

We could scale also the
\varrho
-coordinate. Adjusting accordingly the other variables, we can set without loss of generality
\rho_{0}=1
. In the following we shall not discuss the dependency with respect to
\rho_{1}
, and in particular the physically relevant limit of small density contrast,
\frac{\rho_{1}-\rho_{0}}{\rho_{0}}\ll 1
; see
[
Duchene16
]
and references therein.

\tilde{\bm{x}}=\frac{{\bm{x}}}{\lambda}\quad\text{ and }\quad\tilde{t}=\frac{\sqrt{gH}}{\lambda}t.

Introducing the dimensionless (thickness) diffusion parameter,
\tilde{\kappa}
and the shallowness parameter,
\mu
, through

\tilde{\kappa}=\frac{\kappa}{\lambda\sqrt{gH}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mu=\frac{H^{2}}{\lambda^{2}},

substituting the scaled coordinates/variables in system (
2.2
) and the subsequent equation
and dropping the tildes for the sake of readability
yields

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\cdot\big(({\underline{h}}+h)({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}})\big)

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{{\bm{x}}}h,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\varrho\Big(\partial_{t}{\bm{u}}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}h}{{\underline{h}}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}\Big)+\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}P+\frac{\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\eta}{{\underline{h}}+h}(\varrho{\underline{h}}+\partial_{\varrho}P)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mu\varrho\Big(\partial_{t}w+(\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}w\Big)-\frac{{\partial}_{\varrho}P}{\underline{h}+h}+\frac{\varrho h}{\underline{h}+h}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle-(\underline{h}+h)\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\cdot{\bm{u}}-\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\eta\cdot({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}^{\prime}+\partial_{\varrho}{\bm{u}})+\partial_{\varrho}w

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{(div.-free cond.)}

\displaystyle\eta(\cdot,\varrho)=\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}h(\cdot,\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime},\qquad P\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}=0,\qquad w\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{1}}

\displaystyle=0.\quad\text{(bound. cond.)}

The hydrostatic system is obtained by setting
\mu=0
in (
2.1
). Specifically, plugging the hydrostatic balance

\frac{{\partial}_{\varrho}P}{\underline{h}+h}=\frac{\varrho h}{\underline{h}+h}\quad\text{ and }\quad P\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}=0

into the second equation of (
2.1
) yields

(2.5a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot((\underline{h}+h)({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}))

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}h,

\displaystyle\varrho\Big(\partial_{t}{\bm{u}}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}\Big)+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi

\displaystyle=0,

with

\displaystyle\psi(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)

\displaystyle=\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\varrho}\varrho^{\prime}h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}+\varrho\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}

(2.5b)

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho_{1}}h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}+\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\varrho}\int_{\varrho^{\prime}}^{\rho_{1}}h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho^{\prime\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime\prime}\dd\varrho^{\prime}.

We shall provide a rigorous proof of the convergence of (smooth) solutions to (
2.1
) towards (smooth) solutions to (
2.5
) as
\mu\searrow 0
, under the stable stratification assumption,
\underline{h}+h>0
.

2.2.
Our main results

Our main results are stated and commented below. Some notations, and in particular the Sobolev spaces
H^{s,k}(\Omega)
, are introduced right after. First, we prove the existence, uniqueness and control of the solutions to the hydrostatic system (
2.5
) for sufficiently smooth initial data. Let us point out that the existence time of our solutions encodes the aforementioned stabilizing (resp. destabilizing) effect of the stable stratification and thickness diffusivity (resp. shear velocity).

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
s,k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
s>2+\frac{d}{2}
,
2\leq k\leq s
, and
\underline{M},M,h_{\star},h^{\star}>0
and
0<\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}
be fixed. Then there exists
C>0
such that for any
\kappa\in(0,1]
, any
\underline{h},\underline{{\bm{u}}}\in W^{k,\infty}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))
satisfying

\big\lvert\underline{h}\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}+\big\lvert\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}\big\rvert_{W^{k-1,\infty}_{\varrho}}\leq\underline{M}

and any initial data
(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0})\in H^{s,k}(\Omega)
, with
\eta_{0}(\cdot,\varrho)=\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}h_{0}(\cdot,\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}
, satisfying
the following estimate

M_{0}:=\big\lVert\eta_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}+\big\lvert\eta_{0}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s}_{\bm{x}}}+\kappa^{1/2}\big\lVert h_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq M;

and the stable stratification assumption

\forall({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega,\qquad h_{\star}\leq\underline{h}(\varrho)+h_{0}({\bm{x}},\varrho)\leq h^{\star},

there exists a unique
(h^{\rm h},{\bm{u}}^{\rm h})\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];H^{s,k}(\Omega)^{1+d})
solution to (
2.5
) and
(h^{\rm h},{\bm{u}}^{\rm h})\big|_{t=0}=(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0})
, where

(2.6)

T^{-1}=C\,\big(1+\kappa^{-1}\big(\big\lvert\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}\big\rvert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}}^{2}+M_{0}^{2}\big)\big).

Moreover,
h^{\rm h}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{s+1,k}(\Omega))
and one has, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\forall({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega,\qquad h_{\star}/2\leq\underline{h}(\varrho)+h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)\leq 2\,h^{\star},

and, denoting
\eta^{\rm h}(\cdot,\varrho)=\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}h^{\rm h}(\cdot,\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}
,

\big\lVert\eta^{\rm h}(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}^{\rm h}(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}+\big\lvert\eta^{\rm h}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}(t,\cdot)\big\rvert_{H^{s}_{\bm{x}}}+\kappa^{1/2}\big\lVert h^{\rm h}(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\\
+\kappa^{1/2}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{\rm h}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{s,k})}+\kappa^{1/2}\big\lvert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{\rm h}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{s}_{\bm{x}})}+\kappa\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}h^{\rm h}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{s,k})}\leq CM_{0}.

In our second main result, we prove that within the timescale defined by (
2.6
), there exist solutions to the non-hydrostatic equations (
2.1
) for
\mu
sufficiently small, and they converge towards the corresponding solutions of the hydrostatic equations, with the expected
\mathcal{O}(\mu)
convergence rate.

Theorem 2.2
.

There exists
p\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
k=s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\underline{M},M,h_{\star},h^{\star}>0
and
0<\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}
, there exists
C>0
such that the following holds. For any
0<M_{0}\leq M
,
0<\kappa\leq 1
, and
\mu>0
such that

\mu\leq\kappa/(CM_{0}^{2}),

for any for any
(\underline{h},\underline{{\bm{u}}})\in W^{k+p,\infty}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))^{2}
satisfying

\big\lvert\underline{h}\big\rvert_{W^{k+p,\infty}_{\varrho}}+\big\lvert\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}\big\rvert_{W^{k+p-1,\infty}_{\varrho}}\leq\underline{M}\,;

for any initial data
(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0},w_{0})\in H^{s+p,k+p}(\Omega)^{2+d}
satisfying the boundary condition
w_{0}|_{\varrho=\rho_{1}}=0
and the incompressibility condition

-(\underline{h}+h_{0})\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}_{0}-(\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta_{0})\cdot({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}^{\prime}+\partial_{\varrho}{\bm{u}}_{0})+\partial_{\varrho}w_{0}=0,

(denoting
\eta_{0}(\cdot,\varrho)=\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}h_{0}(\cdot,\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}
), the inequality

\big\lVert\eta_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s+p,k+p}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s+p,k+p}}+\big\lvert\eta_{0}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s+p}_{\bm{x}}}+\kappa^{1/2}\big\lVert h_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s+p,k+p}}=M_{0}\leq M

and the stable stratification assumption

\forall({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega,\qquad h_{\star}\leq\underline{h}(\varrho)+h_{0}({\bm{x}},\varrho)\leq h^{\star},

the following holds. Denoting
(h^{\rm h},{\bm{u}}^{\rm h})\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T^{\rm h}];H^{s+p,k+p}(\Omega)^{1+d})
the solution to the hydrostatic equations (
2.5
) with initial data
(h^{\rm h},{\bm{u}}^{\rm h})\big|_{t=0}=(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0})
provided by Theorem
2.1
,
there exists a unique strong solution
(h^{\rm nh},{\bm{u}}^{\rm nh},w^{\rm nh})\in\mathcal{C}([0,T^{\rm h}];H^{s,k}(\Omega)^{2+d})
to the non-hydrostatic equations (
2.1
) with initial data
(h^{\rm nh},{\bm{u}}^{\rm nh},w^{\rm nh})\big|_{t=0}=(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0},w_{0})
.
Moreover, one has

\big\lVert h^{\rm nh}-h^{\rm h}\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T^{\rm h};H^{s,k})}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}^{\rm nh}-{\bm{u}}^{\rm h}\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T^{\rm h};H^{s,k})}\leq C\,\mu.

2.3.
Strategy of the proofs

Our results rely mainly on the energy method, exhibiting the structure of the systems of equations trough well-chosen energy functionals and making use of product, commutator and composition estimates in the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces
H^{s,k}(\Omega)
that are summarized in the Appendix.

Based on the structure of hydrostatic equations without thickness diffusivity (
\kappa=0
) (
2.5
), namely

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot((\underline{h}+h)({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}))

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\varrho\Big(\partial_{t}{\bm{u}}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}\Big)+\mathcal{M}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi

\displaystyle=0,

where the operator
\mathcal{M}
defining the Montgomery potential in (
2.5b
) is self-adjoint for the
L^{2}_{\varrho}
inner-product and satisfies for all
h_{1},h_{2}\in L^{2}_{\varrho}:=L^{2}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))

\big(\mathcal{M}h_{1}\ ,\ h_{2}\big)_{L^{2}_{\varrho}}=\rho_{0}\Big(\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho_{1}}h_{1}\dd\varrho\Big)\Big(\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho_{1}}h_{2}\dd\varrho\Big)+\Big(\int_{\cdot}^{\rho_{1}}h_{1}\dd\varrho\ ,\ \int_{\cdot}^{\rho_{1}}h_{2}\dd\varrho\Big)_{L^{2}_{\varrho}},

the natural energy functional associated with the hydrostatic equations involves
{\bm{u}}
,
\eta:=\int_{\cdot}^{\rho_{1}}h\dd\varrho
, and
\eta|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}=\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho_{1}}h\dd\varrho
(that represents the free surface deformation), and their derivatives. Notice we do
not
control
h=-{\partial}_{\varrho}\eta
(see
2.1
) in the same regularity class as
{\bm{u}},\eta
unless it is multiplied by the prefactor
\kappa^{1/2}
. In our energy estimates, problematic contributions arise from the commutator between advection operator and density integration in the equation of mass conservation,
i.e.
the first equation in (
2.5
). In order to control these contributions we crucially use the diffusivity-induced regularization, which explains why the lower bound on the time of existence of our solution stated in (
2.6
) vanishes as
\kappa\searrow 0
, yet with a prefactor involving the shear velocity,
\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}(\varrho)
(since advection by a
\varrho
-independent velocity commutes with density integration). It is interesting to notice that the index of regularity with respect to the space variable,
s
, and the one with respect to the density variable,
k
, are decoupled (yet only in the hydrostatic framework). This is due to the fact that the isopycnal change of coordinate is semi-Lagrangian: the advection in isopycnal coordinates occurs only in the horizontal space directions. It would be of utmost interest (but outside of the scope of the present work) to decrease the regularity assumption with respect to the density variable, so as to admit discontinuities, representing density interfaces.

Concerning the non-hydrostatic system, (
2.1
), the natural energy space involves additionally
\sqrt{\mu}w
and its derivatives (hence the control vanishes as
\mu\searrow 0
). In order to obtain suitable energy estimates, we decompose the pressure as the sum of the hydrostatic contribution and the non-hydrostatic contribution, the latter being of lower order in terms of regularity and/or smallness with respect to
\mu\ll 1
. Then we use the structure of the hydrostatic equations, which we complement with an additional symmetric structure for the non-hydrostatic contributions. There, the difficulty consists in providing controls of the energy norms that are uniform with respect to the vanishing parameter
\mu\ll 1
. Our estimates concerning the non-hydrostatic contribution of the pressure stem from elliptic estimates on a boundary-value problem. This strategy is heavily inspired by the work of Desjardins, Lannes and Saut, and it is interesting to compare our results with the analogous “large-time” well-posedness result in
[
Desjardins-Lannes-Saut
]
*Theorem 2.
Firstly, due to the choice of isopycnal coordinates, our boundary-value problem is no longer an anisotropic Poisson equation but involves a fully nonlinear elliptic operator. Since this operator involves
h
that is not controlled in the energy space, we use again the diffusivity-induced regularization at this stage. On the positive side, using isopycnal coordinates rather than Eulerian coordinates allows us to consider the free-surface framework (since isopycnal coordinates readily set the domain as a flat strip, thanks to our assumption that the density is constant at the surface and at the bottom) rather than the rigid-lid setting. We believe that our study can be extended to the rigid-lid framework with small adjustments. Incidentally, we do not employ the often-used Boussinesq approximation, since it is not useful in our context.
Additionally, we do not rely on the use of strong boundary conditions on the initial density and velocities and their derivatives at the surface and the bottom, which instead are assumed in
[
Desjardins-Lannes-Saut
]
(and in most of the other works, often put in a periodic framework) and rather use only the natural no-slip boundary condition at the bottom; the former allow to cancel the trace contributions resulting from vertical integration by parts. We also consider the general situation where the velocity field is a perturbation of a non-zero background current,
\underline{{\bm{u}}}
. In turn, the price to pay to handle this general framework manifests in terms of some restrictions on the length of the time of existence of our solutions, which is inversely proportional with respect to the size of the fluctuations in
[
Desjardins-Lannes-Saut
]
*Theorem 2.

Now we describe our strategy to prove the convergence of the solutions to the non-hydrostatic system towards the hydrostatic one.
First, we point out that a direct use of energy estimates as previously allows to obtain the existence of solutions of the non-hydrostatic equations in a timescale uniform with respect to
\mu
but not necessarily the same as the existence time of the corresponding solution to the hydrostatic equations, and (for technical reasons) restricted to sufficiently small data. To overcome these issues, we look at the hydrostatic solution as an approximate solution to the non-hydrostatic system (in the sense of consistency, as it approximately solves the non-hydrostatic equations), and deduce, using the aforementioned structure of the non-hydrostatic equations, an energy inequality that controls the difference between the solution to the non-hydrostatic system and the (respective) solution to the hydrostatic one. This stratey allows to bootstrap the control of the difference of the two solutions (and hence the control of the solution to the non-hydrostatic equations) within the timescale of existence (in a higher-regularity space) of the hydrostatic solution, provided that the parameter
\mu
is sufficiently small. However, the rate of convergence obtained by this method is not optimal. Therefore, in a second step we implement another strategy to obtain the expected (optimal) convergence rate. It simply consists in taking the opposite viewpoint: to look at the solution to the non-hydrostatic equations as an approximate solution to the hydrostatic equations (again in the sense of consistency) and
use the structure of the hydrostatic equations to infer the
\mathcal{O}(\mu)
convergence rate. Both steps involve loss of derivatives, described by the parameter
p
in Theorem
2.2
.

2.4.
Notation and conventions

We highlight the following conventions used throughout the paper.

•

\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{1}
are fixed constants such that
0<\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}
, and the dependency with respect to these constants is never explicitly displayed.

•

For
k,s\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\leq s
, and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
, we define the functional space

(2.7)

\displaystyle H^{s,k}(\Omega)=\left\{f\ :\ \forall({\bm{\alpha}},j)\in\mathbb{N}^{d+1},\ |{\bm{\alpha}}|+j\leq s,\ j\leq k,\ \partial_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}f\in L^{2}(\Omega)\right\},

endowed with the topology of the norm

(2.8)

\big\lVert f\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\sum_{|{\bm{\alpha}}|=0}^{s-j}\big\lVert\partial_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}f\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}.

When
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
(and
k\in\mathbb{N}
) we define
H^{s^{\prime},k}(\Omega)=\left\{f\ :\ \forall j\in\mathbb{N},\ j\leq k,\ \Lambda^{s^{\prime}-j}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}f\in L^{2}(\Omega)\right\}
and

\big\lVert f\big\rVert_{H^{s^{\prime},k}}^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\big\lVert\Lambda^{s^{\prime}-j}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}f\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}.

where
\Lambda=(\Id-\Delta_{\bm{x}})^{1/2}
. Of course the two notations are consistent when
s^{\prime}=s\in\mathbb{N}
, up to harmless factors in the definition of the norm.

•

We use both the equivalent notations
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=H^{s}_{\bm{x}}
(the usual
L^{2}
-based Sobolev space on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) and
W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=W^{k,\infty}_{\bm{x}}
(the
L^{\infty}
-based Sobolev space on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
), and similarly
L^{2}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))=L^{2}_{\varrho}
and
W^{k,\infty}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))=W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}
. For functions with variables in
\Omega
we denote for instance

L^{2}_{\varrho}L^{\infty}_{\bm{x}}=L^{2}(\rho_{0},\rho_{1};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))=\{f\ :\ \esssup_{{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(\cdot,{\bm{x}})|\in L^{2}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))\}.

Notice
L^{2}_{\varrho}L^{2}_{\bm{x}}=L^{2}_{\bm{x}}L^{2}_{\varrho}=L^{2}(\Omega)
and
L^{\infty}_{\varrho}L^{\infty}_{\bm{x}}=L^{\infty}_{\bm{x}}L^{\infty}_{\varrho}=L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. We use similar notations for functions also depending on time. For instance, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
X
a Banach space as above,
\mathcal{C}^{k}([0,T];X)
is the space of functions with values in
X
which are continuously differentiable up to order
k
, and
L^{p}(0,T;X)
the
p
-integrable
X
-valued functions. All these spaces are endowed with their natural norms.

•

For any operator
A:f\rightarrow Af
, we denote by
[A,f]g=A(fg)-f(Ag)
the usual commutator, while
[A;f,g]=A(fg)-f(Ag)-g(Af)
is the symmetric commutator,

•

C(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)
denotes a constant which depends continuously on its parameters.

•

For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we use the notation
a\lesssim b
(resp.
a\gtrsim b
) if there exists
C>0
, independent of relevant parameters, such that
a\leq Cb
(resp.
a\geq Cb
). We write
a\approx b
if
a\lesssim b
and
a\gtrsim b
.

•

We put
a\vee b:=\max(a,b)
. Finally,

\big\langle B_{a}\big\rangle_{a>b}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }a\leq b\,,\\
B_{a}&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad\big\langle B_{a}\big\rangle_{a=b}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }a\neq b\,,\\
B_{a}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

3.
The hydrostatic system

In this section we study the hydrostatic system in isopycnal coordinates. Specifically, we provide in this section a well-posedness result on the initial-value problem, namely Theorem
2.1
. The result follows from careful
a priori
energy estimates, and the standard method of parabolic regularization. Therefore we will first study the system

(3.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot((\underline{h}+h)({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}))

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}h,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bm{u}}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}+\tfrac{1}{\varrho}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi

\displaystyle=\nu\Delta_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}},

with

\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho):=\rho_{0}\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho_{1}}\nabla_{\bm{x}}h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}+\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\varrho}\int_{\rho^{\prime}}^{\rho_{1}}\nabla_{\bm{x}}h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho^{\prime\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime\prime}\dd\varrho^{\prime},

and
\nu>0
, and will rigorously establish the limit
\nu\rightarrow 0
.

6
6

6

As highlighted by the anonymous referee, another reasonable approach, inspired by the specific form of the viscosity contributions in the shallow water equations advocated in
[
Gent93
]
and analyzed using the energy method in
[
GustafssonSundstroem78
]
, would be to consider the following system

(3.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot((\underline{h}+h)({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}))

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}h,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bm{u}}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}+\tfrac{1}{\varrho}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi

\displaystyle=\nu\frac{\di_{\bm{x}}((\underline{h}+h)\nabla_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}})}{\underline{h}+h}.

Notice that after straightforward computations the system (
3.2
) can be reformulated as

(3.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}h+\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot((\underline{h}+h)({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}))

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}h,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bm{u}}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-(\kappa+\nu)\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}+\tfrac{1}{\varrho}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\psi

\displaystyle=\nu\Delta_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}},

so that the difference with respect to (
3.1
) is minor, and our analysis can easily be extend to (
3.3
).
Specifically, the only significant difference arises in the energy estimates of Lemma
3.5
. Because the first contribution of (v) in the proof vanishes, the assumption on the control of
\nu^{1/2}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}h(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
is no longer necessary.
As a consequence, Proposition
3.6
holds without assuming
\nu\leq\kappa
. This leads to the the interesting problem of the large-time well-posedness of the initial-value problem for (
3.3
) when
\kappa=0
,
\nu>0
, which we believe can be addressed in the spirit of the BD and
\kappa
entropies described in Section
1.2
, but leave open for future studies.

3.1.
Well-posedness of the regularized hydrostatic system

We start with proving the well-posedness of the initial value problem.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
s>\frac{3}{2}+\frac{d}{2}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leq k\leq s
, and
\underline{M}
,
M_{0}
,
h_{\star}
,
\nu
,
\kappa>0
and
C_{0}>1
. Then there exists
T=T(s,k,\underline{M},M_{0},h_{\star},\nu,\kappa,C_{0})
such that
for any
(\underline{h},\underline{{\bm{u}}})=(\underline{h}(\varrho),\underline{{\bm{u}}}(\varrho))\in W^{k,\infty}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))
and for any
(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0})=(h_{0}({\bm{x}},\varrho),{\bm{u}}_{0}({\bm{x}},\varrho))\in H^{s,k}(\Omega)
such that

\inf_{({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega}(\underline{h}(\varrho)+h_{0}({\bm{x}},\varrho))\geq h_{\star},\quad\big\lvert(\underline{h},\underline{{\bm{u}}})\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}\leq\underline{M},\quad\big\lVert(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq M_{0},

there exists a unique solution
(h,{\bm{u}})\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];H^{s,k}(\Omega))
to system (
3.1
) with
(h,{\bm{u}})\big|_{t=0}=(h_{0},{\bm{u}}_{0})
. Moreover,
(h,{\bm{u}})\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{s+1,k}(\Omega))
and, for a universal constant
c_{0}>0
, the following estimates hold

(3.4)

\begin{cases}\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{s,k})}+c_{0}\kappa^{1/2}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}h\big\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{s,k})}<C_{0}\big\lVert h_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}};\\
\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{s,k})}+c_{0}\nu^{1/2}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{s,k})}<C_{0}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}};\\
\inf_{(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)\in(0,T)\times\Omega}(\underline{h}(\varrho)+h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho))>h_{\star}/C_{0}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We will construct the solution as the fixed point of the Duhamel formula

\displaystyle h(t,\cdot)

\displaystyle=e^{\kappa t\Delta_{\bm{x}}}h_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\kappa(t-\tau)\Delta_{\bm{x}}}f(h(\tau,\cdot),{\bm{u}}(\tau,\cdot))\dd\tau,

\displaystyle{\bm{u}}(t,\cdot)

\displaystyle=e^{\nu t\Delta_{\bm{x}}}{\bm{u}}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta_{\bm{x}}}(\bm{f}_{1}+\bm{f}_{2})(h(\tau,\cdot),{\bm{u}}(\tau,\cdot))\dd\tau

where
e^{\alpha t\Delta_{\bm{x}}}
with
\alpha>0
is the heat semigroup defined by
\mathcal{F}[e^{\alpha t\Delta_{\bm{x}}}f](\bm{\xi})=e^{-\alpha t|\bm{\xi}|^{2}}\mathcal{F}[f](\bm{\xi})

where
\mathcal{F}
is the Fourier transform with respect to the variable
{\bm{x}}
, and

\displaystyle f(h,{\bm{u}})

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot((\underline{h}+h)({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}})),

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{1}(h,{\bm{u}})

\displaystyle=-\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{2}(h,{\bm{u}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\varrho}\left(\rho_{0}\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\rho_{1}}\nabla_{\bm{x}}h(\cdot,\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}+\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\varrho}\int_{\rho^{\prime}}^{\rho_{1}}\nabla_{\bm{x}}h(\cdot,\varrho^{\prime\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime\prime}\dd\varrho^{\prime}\right).

Let us first recall the standard regularization properties of the heat flow. For any
\nu>0
,
T>0
and for any
u_{0}\in H^{s,k}(\Omega)
and
g\in L^{1}(0,T;H^{s,k}(\Omega))
, there exists a unique
u\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];H^{s,k}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{s+1,k}(\Omega))
solution to
\partial_{t}u-\nu\Delta_{\bm{x}}u=g
with
u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}
which reads by definition

u=e^{\nu t\Delta_{\bm{x}}}u_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta_{\bm{x}}}g(\tau,\cdot)\dd\tau,

and we have

\big\lVert u\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{s,k})}+c_{0}\nu^{1/2}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}u\big\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{s,k})}\leq\big\lVert u_{0}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}+\big\lVert g\big\rVert_{L^{1}(0,T;H^{s,k})},

where
c_{0}>0
is a universal constant.
The existence and uniqueness of the solution as well as the above estimate easily follow from solving the equation (for almost every
\varrho\in(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
) in Fourier space and using Plancherel’s formula, then using that
\partial_{\varrho}
commutes with
\partial_{t}
and
\Delta_{\bm{x}}
, and invoking Minkowski’s integral inequality (resp. Fubini’s theorem) to exchange the order of integration in the variables
(t,\varrho)
(resp.
({\bm{x}},\varrho)
). We also remark that, by the positivity of the heat kernel and the continuous embedding
H^{s-1,1}(\Omega)\subset L^{\infty}(\Omega)
for
s>\frac{3}{2}+\frac{d}{2}
(see Lemma
A.1
),

\inf_{\Omega}u\geq\inf_{\Omega}u_{0}-\big\lVert g\big\rVert_{L^{1}(0,T;H^{s-1,1})}.

Now we consider
(f,\bm{f}_{1}+\bm{f}_{2})
as a bounded operator from
H^{s+1,k}(\Omega)^{1+d}
to
H^{s,k}(\Omega)^{1+d}
. Indeed, there exists
C_{s,k}>0
such that for any
(h,{\bm{u}})\in H^{s+1,k}(\Omega)^{1+d}
,

\displaystyle\big\lVert f(h,{\bm{u}})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\underline{h}{\bm{u}}+h{\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+h{\bm{u}})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{s,k}\times\left(\big\lvert\underline{h}\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}+\big\lvert\underline{{\bm{u}}}\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}+\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}+\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\right),

where we used straightforward product estimates for the first two terms, and Lemma
A.3
for the last ones. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\big\lVert\bm{f}_{1}(h,{\bm{u}})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}

\displaystyle\leq\big\lVert\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{s,k}\left(\big\lvert\underline{{\bm{u}}}\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\right)\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa C_{s,k}\big(\big\lvert\underline{h}^{-1}\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}+\big\lVert\tfrac{h}{\underline{h}(\underline{h}+h)}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\big)\big\lVert(\nabla_{\bm{x}}h\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}){\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}.

Using the constraint
\inf_{(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})}\underline{h}\geq\inf_{\Omega}(\underline{h}+h_{0})\geq h_{\star}>0
and Lemma
A.6
, we find that for any
h_{\star}>0
and
M_{0},\underline{M}\geq 0
there exists
C_{s,k}(h_{\star},\underline{M},M_{0},C_{0})
such that for any
h\in H^{s,k}(\Omega)
bounded by
\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq C_{0}M_{0}
and satisfying
\inf_{({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega}(\underline{h}(\varrho)+h({\bm{x}},\varrho))\geq h_{\star}/C_{0}
, one has

\big\lvert\underline{h}^{-1}\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}+\big\lVert\tfrac{h}{\underline{h}(\underline{h}+h)}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq C_{s,k}(h_{\star},\underline{M},M_{0},C_{0}).

Using the last estimates in Lemma
A.3
, since
s>\frac{3}{2}+\frac{d}{2}
, we have

\big\lVert(\nabla_{\bm{x}}h\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}){\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}+\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}.

Finally, from the continuous embedding
L^{\infty}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))\subset L^{2}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))\subset L^{1}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))
we immediately infer

\big\lVert\bm{f}_{2}(h,{\bm{u}})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq C_{s,k}\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}.

Altogether, we find that for any
h_{\star},C_{0}>0
and
\underline{M},M_{0}\geq 0
there exists
C_{s,k}(h_{\star},\underline{M},M_{0},C_{0})
such that for any
(h,{\bm{u}})\in H^{s+1,k}(\Omega)^{1+d}
satisfying
\big\lVert(h,{\bm{u}})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq C_{0}M_{0}
and
\inf_{({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega}(\underline{h}(\varrho)+h({\bm{x}},\varrho))\geq h_{\star}/C_{0}
, we have

\big\lVert\big(f(h,{\bm{u}}),\bm{f}_{1}(h,{\bm{u}}),\bm{f}_{2}(h,{\bm{u}})\big)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq C_{s,k}(h_{\star},\underline{M},M_{0},C_{0})\,(1+\kappa)\,\big\lVert(h,{\bm{u}})\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}.

By similar considerations, we find that for any
h_{\star},C_{0}>0
and
\underline{M},M_{0}\geq 0
there exists
C_{s,k}(h_{\star},\underline{M},M_{0},C_{0})
such that for any
(h_{1},{\bm{u}}_{1},h_{2},{\bm{u}}_{2})\in H^{s+1,k}(\Omega)^{2(1+d)}
satisfying the bound
\big\lVert(h_{i},{\bm{u}}_{i})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq C_{0}M_{0}
as well as
\inf_{({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega}(\underline{h}(\varrho)+h_{i}({\bm{x}},\varrho))\geq h_{\star}/C_{0}
(with
i\in\{1,2\}
),
one has

\big\lVert\big(f(h_{2},{\bm{u}}_{2})-f(h_{1},{\bm{u}}_{1}),\bm{f}_{1}(h_{2},{\bm{u}}_{2})-\bm{f}_{1}(h_{1},{\bm{u}}_{1}),\bm{f}_{2}(h_{2},{\bm{u}}_{2})-\bm{f}_{2}(h_{1},{\bm{u}}_{1})\big)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\\
\leq C_{s,k}(h_{\star},\underline{M},M_{0},C_{0})(1+\kappa)\Big(\big\lVert(h_{2}-h_{1},{\bm{u}}_{2}-{\bm{u}}_{1})\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}\\
+\big\lVert(h_{1},h_{2},{\bm{u}}_{1},{\bm{u}}_{2})\big\rVert_{H^{s+1,k}}\big\lVert(h_{2}-h_{1},{\bm{u}}_{2}-{\bm{u}}_{1})\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\Big).

From the above estimates, we easily infer that for
T>0
sufficiently small (uniquely depending on
s,k,M_{0},\underline{M},h_{\star},\nu,\kappa,C_{0}
),

\mathcal{T}:\begin{pmatrix}h\\
{\bm{u}}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}e^{\kappa t\Delta_{\bm{x}}}h_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\kappa(t-\tau)\Delta_{\bm{x}}}f(h(\tau,\cdot),{\bm{u}}(\tau,\cdot))\dd\tau\\
e^{\nu t\Delta_{\bm{x}}}{\bm{u}}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\nu(t-\tau)\Delta_{\bm{x}}}(\bm{f}_{1}+\bm{f}_{2})(h(\tau,\cdot),{\bm{u}}(\tau,\cdot))\dd\tau\end{pmatrix}

is a contraction mapping on

X=\left\{(h,{\bm{u}})\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];H^{s,k}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{s+1,k}(\Omega))\ :\ \text{\eqref{eq:bound-parabolic} holds}\right\}.

The Banach fixed point theorem provides the existence and uniqueness of a fixed point (solution to (
3.1
)) in
X
, and uniqueness in
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];H^{s,k}(\Omega))
is easily checked (for instance by the energy method).
∎

Remark 3.2
.

It should be emphasized that the time of existence provided by Proposition
3.1
is not uniform with respect to the parameters
\kappa,\nu>0
. More precisely, the proof provides a lower bound as

T\gtrsim\min(\{1,\kappa,\nu\}),\qquad\text{i.e.}\quad T^{-1}\lesssim 1+\kappa^{-1}+\nu^{-1}.

3.2.
Quasilinearization

In the result below, we apply spatial derivatives to system (
3.1
) and rewrite it in such a way that the linearized equations satisfied by the highest-order terms exhibit a skew-symmetric structure, which will allow us to obtain improved energy estimates in the subsequent section.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
s,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
s>2+\frac{d}{2}
and
2\leq k\leq s
, and
\underline{M},M,h_{\star}>0
. Then there exists
C=C(s,k,\underline{M},M,h_{\star})>0
such that for any
\kappa\in[0,1]
,
\nu\geq 0
, for any

(\underline{h},\underline{{\bm{u}}})\in W^{k,\infty}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))
such that

\big\lvert\underline{h}\big\rvert_{W^{k,\infty}_{\varrho}}+\big\lvert\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}\big\rvert_{W^{k-1,\infty}_{\varrho}}\leq\underline{M}\,;

and any
(h,{\bm{u}})\in L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{s,k}(\Omega))
solution to (
3.1
) with some
T>0
and satisfying for almost every
t\in[0,T]

\big\lVert h(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{H^{s-1,k-1}}+\big\lVert\eta(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}+\big\lvert\eta(t,\cdot)\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s}_{\bm{x}}}+\kappa^{1/2}\big\lVert h(t,\cdot)\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\leq M

(where
\eta(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho):=\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho^{\prime})\dd\varrho^{\prime}
) and

\inf_{({\bm{x}},\varrho)\in\Omega}\underline{h}(\varrho)+h(t,{\bm{x}},\varrho)\geq h_{\star},

the following holds. Denote, for any multi-index
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
,
\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}={\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}}\eta,\,{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}={\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}}{\bm{u}}
.

•

For any
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
with
0\leq|{\bm{\alpha}}|\leq s
, we have that

(3.5a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+(\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}(\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}+{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\,d{\varrho^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}(\underline{h}+h)\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\dd\varrho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big){\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}

\displaystyle+\frac{\rho_{0}}{\varrho}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}+\frac{1}{\varrho}\int_{\rho_{0}}^{\varrho}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\dd\varrho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu\Delta_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+{\bm{R}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},0},

where for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,
(R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}(t,\cdot),{\bm{R}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}(t,\cdot))\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([\rho_{0},\rho_{1}];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and

(3.5b)

\displaystyle\big\lVert R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\big\lVert{\bm{R}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\big\lvert R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\,M\,\big(1+\kappa\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}h\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\big).

•

For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq j\leq k

and any
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
,
0\leq|{\bm{\alpha}}|\leq s-j
, it holds

(3.6a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+(\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+R_{{\bm{\alpha}},j},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+\big(({\underline{{\bm{u}}}}+{\bm{u}}-\kappa\tfrac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}h}{\underline{h}+h})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\big)\partial_{\varrho}^{j}{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}

\displaystyle=\nu\Delta_{\bm{x}}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+{\bm{R}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},j},

where for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,
(R_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}(t,\cdot),{\bm{R}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}(t,\cdot))\in L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
and

(3.6b)

\big\lVert R_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\big\lVert{\bm{R}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\,M\,\big(1+\kappa\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}h\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}\big).

•

For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq j\leq k
and any multi-index
{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
,
0\leq|{\bm{\alpha}}|\leq s-j
, it holds

(3.7a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}h^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+(\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}h^{({\bm{\alpha}})}

\displaystyle=\kappa\Delta_{\bm{x}}\partial_{\varrho}^{j}h^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+r_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}+\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\cdot{\bm{r}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},j},

where for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,
(r_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}(t,\cdot),{\bm{r}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}(t,\cdot))\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{1+d}
and

(3.7b)

\kappa^{1/2}\big\lVert r_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\big\lVert{\bm{r}}_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\,M.

Proof.

In this proof, we denote
s_{0}=s-2>\frac{d}{2}
.

Estimate of
R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}
.
First we notice the identity by integration by parts in
\varrho
,

(\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}+\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}(\underline{{\bm{u}}}^{\prime}+{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\,d{\varrho^{\prime}}=\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}(\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}h^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\dd\varrho^{\prime}.

Hence, recalling the notation
[P;u,v]=P(uv)-u(Pv)-v(Pu)
and integrating by parts in
\varrho
, we get

\displaystyle R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}

\displaystyle:=-\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}},{\bm{u}}]\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}h+[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}},h]\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}\,\dd\varrho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{u}},\nabla_{\bm{x}}h]+{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}h+[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};h,\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}]+h^{({\bm{\alpha}})}(\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}})\,\dd\varrho^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{u}},\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta]-\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{\varrho}^{\rho_{1}}[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}},\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta]+{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}h+[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};h,\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}]+\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}}\,\dd\varrho^{\prime}.

By the standard Sobolev embedding
H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and Lemma
A.1
, one gets

\displaystyle\big\lVert\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\big\lVert\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\lesssim\big\lVert\eta\big\rVert_{H^{s,0}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+\frac{3}{2},1}}.

and

\displaystyle\big\lvert\big(\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}\big)\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lvert\eta\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+\frac{3}{2},1}}.

By Lemma
A.7
(
3
), and Lemma
A.1
, we have

\displaystyle\big\lVert[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{u}},\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta]\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\varrho}H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{\varrho}H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2},1}}\big\lVert\eta\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+2,0}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+\frac{3}{2},1}}\big\lVert\eta\big\rVert_{H^{s,0}},

\displaystyle\big\lvert[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};{\bm{u}}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}},\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}]\big\rvert_{L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lvert{\bm{u}}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lvert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}+\big\lvert{\bm{u}}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lvert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s-\frac{1}{2},1}}\big\lvert\eta\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s_{0}+2}_{\bm{x}}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+\frac{3}{2},1}}\big\lvert\eta\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s}_{\bm{x}}},

and using additionally the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\big\lVert[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}},\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta]\big\rVert_{L^{1}_{\varrho}L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}+\big\lVert{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\eta\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,1}}\big\lVert\eta\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+2,0}}+\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+2,1}}\big\lVert\eta\big\rVert_{H^{s,0}},

\displaystyle\big\lVert[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}};h,\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}]\big\rVert_{L^{1}_{\varrho}L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}+\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s_{0}+1}_{\bm{x}}}\big\lVert\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{L^{2}_{\varrho}H^{s-1}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s-1,0}}{\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+2,0}}}+\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+1,0}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,0}},

and

\displaystyle\big\lVert{\bm{u}}^{({\bm{\alpha}})}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{x}}h\big\rVert_{L^{1}_{\varrho}L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,0}}\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+1,0}},

\displaystyle\big\lVert\eta^{({\bm{\alpha}})}(\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot{\partial}_{\varrho}{\bm{u}})\big\rVert_{L^{1}_{\varrho}L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\lVert\eta\big\rVert_{H^{s,0}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+2,1}}.

Altogether, using the continuous embedding
L^{\infty}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))\subset L^{2}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))\subset L^{1}((\rho_{0},\rho_{1}))
, the Minkowski and triangle inequalities and
s\geq s_{0}+2
, we get

(3.8)

\big\lvert R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{L^{2}_{\bm{x}}}+\big\lVert R_{{\bm{\alpha}},0}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim(\big\lVert\eta\big\rVert_{H^{s,0}}+\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s-1,0}}+\big\lvert\eta\big|_{\varrho=\rho_{0}}\big\rvert_{H^{s}_{\bm{x}}})\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,1}}.

Estimate of
R_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}
for
1\leq j\leq k
.
We have

\displaystyle R_{{\bm{\alpha}},j}

\displaystyle:=-[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial}_{\varrho}^{j-1}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot,\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}}]h-{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{{\bm{\alpha}}}{\partial}_{\varrho}^{j-1}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\cdot(\underline{h}(\underline{{\bm{u}}}+{\bm{u}}))

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{d}[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{{\bm{\alpha}}}\partial_{x_{i}}{\partial}_{\varrho}^{j-1},{\bm{u}}_{i}]h-[{\partial}_{\varrho}^{j-1},\underline{{\bm{u}}}]\cdot{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{\bm{\alpha}}\nabla_{\bm{x}}h-{\partial}_{\varrho}^{j-1}\cdot(\underline{h}{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{{\bm{\alpha}}}\nabla_{\bm{x}}{\bm{u}}),

where
{\bm{u}}_{i}
is the
i^{\rm th}
component of
{\bm{u}}
.
By Lemma
A.8
and since
(|{\bm{\alpha}}|+1)+(j-1)\leq s
and
j-1\leq k-1
, and
s\geq s_{0}+\frac{3}{2}
, we find for
2\leq k-1\leq s

\big\lVert[{\partial}_{\bm{x}}^{{\bm{\alpha}}}\partial_{x_{i}}{\partial}_{\varrho}^{j-1},{\bm{u}}_{i}]h\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\lesssim\big\lVert h\big\rVert_{H^{s-1,k-1}}\big\lVert{\bm{u}}\big\rVert_{H^{s,k}}.

There remains to consider
1\leq j\leq k\leq 2
. If
j=1
we have by Lemma
A.7
(
2
) and since
|{\bm{\alpha}}|\leq s-1
